=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an integer array nums and a positive integer k. Return the sum of the maximum and minimum elements of all subsequences of nums with at most k elements.
Since the answer may be very large, return it modulo 10^9 + 7.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,3], k = 2
Output: 24
Explanation:
The subsequences of nums with at most 2 elements are:



Subsequence 
Minimum
Maximum
Sum


[1]
1
1
2


[2]
2
2
4


[3]
3
3
6


[1, 2]
1
2
3


[1, 3]
1
3
4


[2, 3]
2
3
5


Final Total
 
 
24



The output would be 24.

Example 2:

Input: nums = [5,0,6], k = 1
Output: 22
Explanation: 
For subsequences with exactly 1 element, the minimum and maximum values are the element itself. Therefore, the total is 5 + 5 + 0 + 0 + 6 + 6 = 22.

Example 3:

Input: nums = [1,1,1], k = 2
Output: 12
Explanation:
The subsequences [1, 1] and [1] each appear 3 times. For all of them, the minimum and maximum are both 1. Thus, the total is 12.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 10^5
0 <= nums[i] <= 10^9
1 <= k <= min(70, nums.length)

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

MOD = 10**9+7
class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        nums.sort()
        n=len(nums)
        max_t = k-1
        inv = [0]*(max_t+1)
        if max_t >=1:
            inv[1]=1
            for i in range(2,max_t+1):
                inv[i] = MOD - MOD//i * inv[MOD%i] % MOD
        # Or pow for each? Use formula.
        prefix_counts = [0]*n
        for m in range(n):
            lim = min(max_t, m)
            s = 1  # C(m,0)
            c = 1
            for t in range(1, lim+1):
                c = c * (m - t + 1) % MOD
                c = c * inv[t] % MOD
                s += c
                if s >= MOD: s -= MOD? c mod, s can exceed 2MOD? s up to MOD-1 + MOD-1 = 2MOD-2, one subtraction enough if we maintain s < MOD. But after adding c, if s>=MOD subtract. c < MOD. Good.
            prefix_counts[m] = s % MOD? If s maintained <MOD.
        ans=0
        for i, val in enumerate(nums):
            ans = (ans + (val % MOD) * ((prefix_counts[i] + prefix_counts[n-1-i]) % MOD)) % MOD
        return ans

Could optimize by precomputing `inv` as local variable. In code:
MOD = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        nums.sort()
        n = len(nums)
        r = k - 1
        inv = [0] * (r + 1)
        if r >= 1:
            inv[1] = 1
            for i in range(2, r + 1):
                inv[i] = MOD - (MOD // i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD

        pref = [0] * n
        for m in range(n):
            lim = r if r < m else m
            s = 1
            c = 1
            for t in range(1, lim + 1):
                c = (c * (m - t + 1)) % MOD
                c = (c * inv[t]) % MOD
                s += c
                if s >= MOD:
                    s -= MOD
            pref[m] = s

        ans = 0
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            cnt = pref[i] + pref[n - 1 - i]
            if cnt >= MOD:
                cnt -= MOD
            ans = (ans + (x % MOD) * cnt) % MOD
        return ans

Need check `cnt = pref[i] + pref[n-1-i]`; both < MOD, sum < 2MOD, one subtraction enough. Good.

But wait: `pref[m]` for m maybe >? n=1e5, r<=69. For m=0, lim=0, s=1. For m=1, lim=1 if r>=1. If k=1 r=0, lim=0, s=1. Good.

Let's think if there is any hidden issue with sorting and using "rightmost selected" for max and "leftmost selected" for min simultaneously. We compute sum max using rightmost index in sorted order and sum min using leftmost index. For each subset, max contribution counted once by rightmost selected index; min contribution counted once by leftmost selected index. We add both. Does sorting with duplicates and rightmost/leftmost tie-breaking count each subset exactly once for max? Yes, because every non-empty subset has a unique rightmost index in sorted order. Its value is maximum (since sorted nondecreasing). For min, unique leftmost index. Good.

If there are equal elements after i, i is not rightmost if selected with them; those subsets counted for later index. If equal before i, i can be rightmost and max value same; counted once. Good.

For min: i is leftmost selected; other elements from indices > i. Count C(n-1-i,t). Good.

We need S_m = sum_{t=0}^{min(r,m)} C(m,t), where r=k-1.

For m <= r: S_m = 2^m.

For m > r: S_m = sum_{t=0}^{r} C(m,t).

Can we update S_m from S_{m-1} and C(m-1,r)? Earlier:
S_m = 2*S_{m-1} - C(m-1,r) for m > r? Let's verify for m = r+1:
S_{r+1} = sum_{t=0}^r C(r+1,t) = 2^{r+1} - 1 (since omit C(r+1,r+1)=1).
S_r = 2^r. C(r,r)=1. 2*S_r - C(r,r) = 2^{r+1}-1. Good.
For m > r+1, formula holds? Derive:
C(m,t)=C(m-1,t)+C(m-1,t-1)
S_m = sum_{t=0}^r C(m-1,t) + sum_{t=0}^r C(m-1,t-1)
= S_{m-1} + sum_{u=-1}^{r-1} C(m-1,u)
= S_{m-1} + S_{m-1} - C(m-1,r) (since C(m-1,-1)=0)
= 2*S_{m-1} - C(m-1,r). Yes.

Need maintain C(m-1,r) for m > r. Let's call B_m = C(m, r) maybe. For m >= r. We can update B_m = C(m,r) from B_{m-1}? C(m,r) = C(m-1,r) * m / (m-r). Because C(m,r) = C(m-1,r) * m/(m-r). For m > r. pref = [0]*n
pow2 = 1

# pref[m] = 2^m mod

Option 1:
pref = [0]*n
cur = 1  # S_0
for m in range(n):
    if m <= r:
        if m == 0:
            cur = 1
        else:
            cur = (cur * 2) % MOD  # since S_m = 2^m
        pref[m] = cur
    else:
        # at m = r+1 first, need C_prev = C(m-1, r) = C(r,r)=1
        # But if we didn't set, set when m == r+1.
        if m == r + 1:
            c_prev = 1  # C(r, r)
        # S_m = 2*S_{m-1} - C(m-1,r)
        cur = (2 * pref[m-1] - c_prev) % MOD
        pref[m] = cur
        # update c_prev to C(m, r) for next iteration
        # C(m,r) = C(m-1,r) * m / (m-r)
        c_prev = c_prev * m % MOD * inv[m - r] % MOD

Let's write O(n) code:

from typing import List

MOD = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        nums.sort()
        n = len(nums)
        r = k - 1

        inv = [0] * (n + 1)
        if n >= 1:
            inv[1] = 1
        for i in range(2, n + 1):
            inv[i] = MOD - (MOD // i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD

        pref = [0] * n
        cur = 1
        c_prev = 0
        for m in range(n):
            if m <= r:
                if m == 0:
                    cur = 1
                else:
                    cur = (cur * 2) % MOD
                pref[m] = cur
            else:
                if m == r + 1:
                    c_prev = 1  # C(r, r)
                cur = (2 * pref[m - 1] - c_prev) % MOD
                pref[m] = cur
                c_prev = c_prev * m % MOD * inv[m - r] % MOD

        ans = 0
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            cnt = pref[i] + pref[n - 1 - i]
            if cnt >= MOD:
                cnt -= MOD
            ans = (ans + (x % MOD) * cnt) % MOD
        return ans

Let's write final code:

```python
from typing import List

MOD = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        nums.sort()
        n = len(nums)
        r = k - 1

        inv = [0] * (n + 1)
        inv[1] = 1
        for i in range(2, n + 1):
            inv[i] = MOD - (MOD // i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD

        pref = [0] * n
        cur = 1
        c_prev = 0

        for m in range(n):
            if m <= r:
                if m == 0:
                    cur = 1
                else:
                    cur = (cur * 2) % MOD
                pref[m] = cur
            else:
                if m == r + 1:
                    c_prev = 1  # C(r, r)
                cur = (2 * pref[m - 1] - c_prev) % MOD
                pref[m] = cur
                c_prev = (c_prev * m) % MOD
                c_prev = (c_prev * inv[m - r]) % MOD

        ans = 0
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            cnt = pref[i] + pref[n - 1 - i]
            if cnt >= MOD:
                cnt -= MOD
            ans = (ans + (x % MOD) * cnt) % MOD

        return ans
```

S_m = sum_{t=0}^{r} C(m,t) for m > r.
C(m,t) = C(m-1,t) + C(m-1,t-1)
Sum t=0..r C(m,t) = sum_{t=0}^r C(m-1,t) + sum_{t=0}^r C(m-1,t-1)
= S_{m-1} + sum_{u=-1}^{r-1} C(m-1,u)
= S_{m-1} + (S_{m-1} - C(m-1,r)) because C(m-1,-1)=0 and sum u=0..r-1 = S_{m-1} - C(m-1,r). Yes. So S_m = 2*S_{m-1} - C(m-1,r). Good.

Update C(m,r):
C(m,r) = C(m-1,r) * m / (m-r). Let's verify identity:
C(m,r) = m!/(r!(m-r)!)
C(m-1,r) = (m-1)!/(r!(m-1-r)!)
Ratio = m/(m-r). Yes. Good.
So c_prev = c_prev * m * inv[m-r] mod. Good.

Let's maybe write final code with comments:
```python
from typing import List

MOD = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        nums.sort()
        n = len(nums)
        r = k - 1

        # modular inverses up to n
        inv = [0] * (n + 1)
        inv[1] = 1
        for i in range(2, n + 1):
            inv[i] = MOD - (MOD // i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD

        # pref[m] = sum_{t=0}^{min(r,m)} C(m,t) mod MOD
        pref = [0] * n
        pref[0] = 1
        c_prev = 1  # C(r, r), used when m > r

        for m in range(1, n):
            if m <= r:
                pref[m] = (pref[m - 1] * 2) % MOD
            else:
                pref[m] = (2 * pref[m - 1] - c_prev) % MOD
                c_prev = (c_prev * m) % MOD
                c_prev = (c_prev * inv[m - r]) % MOD

        ans = 0
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            cnt = pref[i] + pref[n - 1 - i]
            if cnt >= MOD:
                cnt -= MOD
            ans = (ans + (x % MOD) * cnt) % MOD

        return ans
```
Need check `pref[0] = 1` if n>=1. Yes.

What if n=1, pref[0]=1, loop range(1,1) none. Good.

Now, let's think about if `c_prev` initialized to 1 but if r > n-1? no. If r=0, C(r,r)=C(0,0)=1. Good.

Now, let's consider if `pref[m]` for m <= r should be `2^m` but if r=69, `pref[m-1]*2` mod. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is a possible problem with sorting and using sorted values but original subsequences count by indices; sorting changes which indices are "previous" and "next" but as argued value multiset counts same. Let's maybe prove with a more complex duplicate example to be absolutely sure:
Original [1,2,1,2], sorted [1,1,2,2]. Count of subsequences with value multiset {1,2}: original freq1=2, freq2=2 -> C(2,1)*C(2,1)=4. Sorted same. Max/min same. Good. Our sorted contribution counts each sorted subset with multiset {1,2} exactly? Let's see sorted positions: 1a,1b,2a,2b. Subsets with one 1 one 2: (1a,2a),(1a,2b),(1b,2a),(1b,2b). For max contribution, max value 2; rightmost selected is one of 2a/2b. For subset (1a,2a), rightmost index 2a (if positions 0,1,2,3; 2a at index2) counted at i=2 with other from previous indices <2: previous indices 0,1 (both 1s) choose one? Wait subset (1a,2a) includes previous index0, yes counted. Subset (1b,2a) includes previous index1, counted. Subset (1a,2b) includes previous indices 0 and 2? Actually 2b index3, previous indices 0,1,2; choose one 1 and maybe not 2a? To have rightmost index3, subset can include 2a? If includes 2a (index2) and 2b (index3), rightmost is 2b, but then multiset has two 2s, not {1,2}. For {1,2} with 2b, choose one 1 from previous indices 0,1 and not 2a. Counted at i=3 with other elements from previous indices choose one 1; there are two such subsets. Good. So each sorted subset counted once. Original count same. Good.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

MOD = 10**9 + 7

class Solution:
    def minMaxSums(self, nums: List[int], k: int) -> int:
        nums.sort()
        n = len(nums)
        r = k - 1

        # Modular inverses up to n
        inv = [0] * (n + 1)
        inv[1] = 1
        for i in range(2, n + 1):
            inv[i] = MOD - (MOD // i) * inv[MOD % i] % MOD

        # pref[m] = sum_{t=0}^{min(r, m)} C(m, t) mod MOD
        pref = [0] * n
        pref[0] = 1
        c_prev = 1  # C(r, r), used when m > r

        for m in range(1, n):
            if m <= r:
                pref[m] = (pref[m - 1] * 2) % MOD
            else:
                pref[m] = (2 * pref[m - 1] - c_prev) % MOD
                c_prev = (c_prev * m) % MOD
                c_prev = (c_prev * inv[m - r]) % MOD

        ans = 0
        for i, x in enumerate(nums):
            cnt = pref[i] + pref[n - 1 - i]
            if cnt >= MOD:
                cnt -= MOD
            ans = (ans + (x % MOD) * cnt) % MOD

        return ans
```
